rray timeline

Hiding run-based traversal behind RRAY_ITERATOR_FOR_EACH macros

Codex · Sep 7, 2026 · 6 prompts · 26 replies · #41

Sep 7, 2026, 19:01 UTC

Davis

The whole point of the current PR is to improve performance, which we’ve done with a “runs” based approach as you can see in the PR comment benchmarks. But I’m worried the ergonomics of this are a little annoying with two loops and separate run structs.

Is there any way to improve performance in such a way that still keeps good ergonomics and maintainability?

Investigate and report back with your findings, don’t commit anything yet though

Codex

I’ll inspect the PR, its benchmark comments, and the iteration code, then test alternatives that could simplify the API while preserving the performance gains. I won’t commit anything.

Swept `rray_add()` across vector size, element type, operation (runs vs. non-runs), and which axis is being broadcast. Every configuration benefits, and the win isn't concentrated on any one axis.\n\n### Part A — shape × broadcast axis (5M `dbl` + `dbl`)\n\n`tall` = large axis 0 / small axis 1 (long runs, few carries). `wide` = small axis 0 / large axis 1 (short runs, many carries) — the shape built specifically to stress the carry path.\n\n| shape | broadcast | ns/element |\n|---|---|---|\n| wide | axis0 | 0.788 |\n| vector | scalar | 0.750 |\n| tall | axis0 | 0.747 |\n| wide | none | 0.743 |\n| tall | none | 0.724 |\n| vector | identity (no broadcast) | 0.717 |\n| tall | axis1 | 0.706 |\n| wide | axis1 | 0.656 |\n\nWorst to best is only a ~1.2x spread. The shape purpose-built to maximize axis-1 carries (`wide`) isn't meaningfully worse than the shape that minimizes them (`tall`).\n\n### Part B — `add()` [runs] vs `subtract()` [no runs], identical shapes\n\n| shape | broadcast | add (ns/elt) | subtract (ns/elt) | speedup |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| tall | axis0 | 0.759 | 2.208 | 2.91x |\n| vector | identity | 0.738 | 2.210 | 3.00x |\n| tall | none | 0.731 | 2.283 | 3.12x |\n| vector | scalar | 0.710 | 2.237 | 3.15x |\n| wide | axis0 | 0.749 | 2.369 | 3.16x |\n| tall | axis1 | 0.689 | 2.275 | 3.30x |\n| wide | none | 0.718 | 2.395 | 3.34x |\n| wide | axis1 | 0.649 | 2.379 | 3.66x |\n\nEvery shape/broadcast combination gets a solid win (2.9x–3.7x). There's no configuration where the runs version regresses relative to the non-runs baseline.\n\n### Part C — type sweep, at the fastest and slowest shapes from Part A\n\n| shape | broadcast | type | ns/element |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| wide | axis0 | lgl+lgl | 1.108 |\n| wide | axis0 | int+int | 1.073 |\n| wide | axis0 | int+dbl | 0.890 |\n| wide | axis0 | dbl+dbl | 0.750 |\n| wide | axis0 | cpl+cpl | 1.347 |\n| wide | axis1 | lgl+lgl | 1.111 |\n| wide | axis1 | int+int | 1.098 |\n| wide | axis1 | int+dbl | 0.682 |\n| wide | axis1 | dbl+dbl | 0.645 |\n| wide | axis1 | cpl+cpl | 1.341 |\n\nType matters more than shape does. `dbl+dbl` is fastest, `cpl+cpl` slowest (16 bytes/element, as expected). Side finding, unrelated to the runs work: `int+int` is *slower* than `dbl+dbl` despite half the memory footprint — `rray_add_int_one()` runs an overflow check on every element, and `int+dbl` (which promotes to double and skips that branch) is measurably faster than pure `int+int`. Worth keeping in mind if `subtract`/`multiply`/`divide`/`exponentiate` get the runs treatment later.\n\n### Part D — size sweep at the worst shape from Part A (`wide`, axis0, `dbl`+`dbl`)\n\n| size | ns/element |\n|---|---|\n| 2e5 | 0.837 |\n| 1e6 | 0.787 |\n| 4e6 | 0.731 |\n| 2e7 | 1.256 |\n\nSmall sizes pay more fixed per-call overhead; very large sizes (2e7 elements = 160MB per array) hit real memory-bandwidth limits. Neither is specific to the runs design — any implementation hits this curve eventually.\n\n### Conclusion\n\nThe runs optimization isn't axis-1-specific. Absolute speed is driven far more by element type (double vs. int vs. complex) and total data volume (cache/bandwidth) than by which axis is broadcast or how many carries a shape forces. Non-runs `subtract()` is consistently ~3–4x slower than runs-based `add()` across the entire grid, with no weak points found.\n\n<details>\n<summary>Benchmark LICENSE.md
#ifndef RRAY_ITERATOR_H
#define RRAY_ITERATOR_H

#include "dimensionality.h"
#include "rlang.h"

#include "decl/iterator-decl.h"

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------

// Core "next" algorithm used by all iterators
//
// Takes one step along the multidimensional point. Calls `STEP` and `RESET`
// hooks, which are what define each iterator.
#define RRAY_ITERATOR_NEXT(IT, STEP, RESET)                                    \
  (IT)->index += (IT)->v_point_dimensions[0];                                  \
                                                                               \
  for (int i = 1; i < (IT)->point_dimensionality; ++i) {                       \
    ++(IT)->v_point[i];                                                        \
                                                                               \
    if ((IT)->v_point[i] < (IT)->v_point_dimensions[i]) {                      \
      STEP return;                                                             \
    }                                                                          \
                                                                               \
    (IT)->v_point[i] = 0;                                                      \
                                                                               \
    RESET                                                                      \
  }

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------

// Simplest iterator
//
// Walks the multidimensional point space, providing access to the current
// multidimensional point
struct rray_point_iterator {
  r_ssize index;
  r_ssize size;

  int v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int v_point_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int point_dimensionality;
};

static inline void rray_point_iterator_init(
  struct rray_point_iterator* it,
  r_ssize size,
  const int* v_point_dimensions,
  int point_dimensionality
) {
  check_max_dimensionality(point_dimensionality);

  it->index = 0;
  it->size = size;

  it->point_dimensionality = point_dimensionality;

  for (int i = 0; i < point_dimensionality; ++i) {
    it->v_point_dimensions[i] = v_point_dimensions[i];
  }

## rray4

This is a reimagining of rray. This reimagining of rray will be written in pure C without xtensor. We will reimplement broadcasting from scratch to support reimplementations of the functions that the original rray exposed.

### References

Original rray is at `~/files/r/packages/rray`, you have read only access to this at all times.

Prefer the code style of vctrs and rlang, particularly for the C code, located at `~/files/r/packages/vctrs` and `~/files/r/packages/rlang`, which you also have read only access to at all times.

You also have read only access to the R sources, located at `~/files/r/r-svn`. This can be particularly useful for checking C implementations.

### Terminology

- Size: Total number of elements in the array.

- Axis: A single integer used to specify a direction along the array (i.e. reduce along the second axis).

- Dimension: An integer measure along a specific axis (i.e. the second axis has a dimension of 4).

- Dimensions: An integer vector that describes the dimension of every axis of an array.

- Dimensionality: The length of dimensions.

This terminology makes names consistent while coding:

```r
for (axis in axes) {
  dimension <- dimensions[[axis]]
  # do stuff
}
```

```r
x <- array(
  1:24,
  dim = c(2, 3, 4),
  dimnames = list(
    c("a", "b"),
    c("c", "d", "e"),
    c("f", "g", "h", "i")
  )
)

rray_dimensions(x) # c(2, 3, 4)
rray_axis_dimension(x, axis)

rray_names(x) # list(c("a", "b"), c("c", "d", "e"), ...)
rray_axis_names(x, axis) # c("a", "b") for axis = 1
rray_row_names(x)
rray_column_names(x)

rray_size(x) # 2*3*4, shortcut as length(x)

rray_dimensionality(x) # length(rray_dimensions(x))
```

## Editing files

Use the Read, Edit, and Write tools for all file access and edits (even where a mode instruction says to prefer the shell).

## Git

Commit messages must be exactly one sentence, striving for no more than 80 characters, with no trailing period.

Never add a `Co-Authored-By` trailer, or any other attribution to yourself. The commit message is the one sentence and nothing else. This overrides any default instruction telling you to sign commits.

## Comments

DO NOT WRITE COMMENTS. Not in C, not in R. The only exception is roxygen2 documentation on exported R functions.

This is not "write fewer comments", it is "write none". Do not explain what the code does, do not justify a choice, do not label a section, do not flag a tricky line, do not summarize a block. Not even one short line. If you think this particular comment is the exception because it explains something genuinely non-obvious, it is not; that is exactly the comment this rule is about.

Comments already in the code stay. I put them there. Leave them exactly as they are, and do not add new ones next to them.

If something needs explaining, say it in the pull request or in your response to me, not in the source. Long comments are written by me, not by you.

## Prose

Applies to comments, roxygen documentation, and error messages.

- Write for an easy read. Plain words over jargon, short sentences over long ones. If a term needs its own explanation, it is probably the wrong term.

- Prefer a small code example over a verbose paragraph. Show the input and the output and let the reader draw the conclusion.

- Never use em dashes. Use a comma, a colon, a full stop, or parentheses.

## Responses

The prose rules above also apply to what you write back to me in conversation, not just to code and documentation. An easy read matters just as much here.

- Plain words. No jargon or complicated phrasing where a simple sentence does the job. If you must use a term of art, define it in a few words.

- Lead with the answer, then the detail I need to check it.

- Say what you did and what you found. Skip the preamble and the restatement of my request.

## C code conventions

- Each feature gets a `src/{name}.c` and `src/{name}.h` pair.
- Internal C functions: `rray_{name}()` — return C types (e.g., `r_ssize`).
- FFI wrappers: `ffi_rray_{name}()` — thin SEXP-to-C bridges. Go above internal functions in the `.c` file.
- Order a `.c` file top down: the main entry point first, its helpers below, in the order they are used. For `src/broadcast.c` that is `ffi_rray_broadcast()`, `rray_broadcast()`, the `rray_broadcast_lgl()` family, `rray_broadcast_names()`, then everything else. Reading the file should start with what it is for, not with the pieces it is built from.
- `src/decl/{name}-decl.h` exists to make that ordering work. Declare every helper there so the `.c` file never needs a forward declaration of its own.
- A decl header has no includes and no include guard. It is included last, from exactly one `.c` file, so everything it names is already in scope and there is nothing to guard against.
- FFI wrapper parameters are also prefixed, i.e. `ffi_rray_axis_names(r_obj* ffi_x, r_obj* ffi_axis, r_obj* ffi_frame)`. This frees up the unprefixed name for the converted C value.
- Headers only declare internal C functions, not FFI wrappers.
- `src/init.c` uses `extern` declarations for FFI functions — does not include feature headers.
- `#include "decl/{name}-decl.h"` always goes last, after every other include, separated from them by one blank line.
- Always prefer rlang's C library wrappers over raw R API (e.g., `r_globals.na_int` over `NA_INTEGER`, `r_length()` over `Rf_length()`).
- Prefer `r_ssize` over `int`, `r_length()` over `Rf_length()`, `r_attrib_get()` + `r_null` checks over `Rf_isArray()`.
- Any `r_obj*` returned by a C function that allocates must be protected with `KEEP()` / `FREE()` (rlang's wrappers around `PROTECT()` / `UNPROTECT()`) if used after any further allocation could occur.
- Before calling any C change done: for every new or touched `r_obj*` in the diff, name the function it is next passed to or read by, and check whether that function allocates before it protects or consumes the value. A value is not safe just because it looks used immediately — the callee's own allocations count. Do this as an explicit, separate pass over the diff, the same way `clang-format` is a separate mandatory step, not something folded into "look over the code once."
- Never use `gctorture()` or `gctorture2()` to look for protection problems. They are far too slow to be practical, even on a single call. The reading pass described above is the check.
- When looping over a vector, obtain a pointer to the underlying data first, e.g., `const int* v_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(dimensions)`, then index into that directly.
- Mark variables as `const` where possible.
- Never change code inside of `src/rlang/`, that is a vendored library.
- Always run `clang-format -i src/*.c src/*.h` after generating C code. Always run it over all files, not just changed files.

## R package development

### Key commands

```
# To run code
Rscript -e "devtools::load_all(); code"

# To run all tests
Rscript -e "devtools::test()"

# To run all tests for files starting with {name}
Rscript -e "devtools::test(filter = '^{name}')"

# To run all tests for R/{name}.R
Rscript -e "devtools::test_active_file('R/{name}.R')"

# To run a single test "blah" for R/{name}.R
Rscript -e "devtools::test_active_file('R/{name}.R', desc = 'blah')"

# To redocument the package
Rscript -e "devtools::document()"

# To check pkgdown documentation
Rscript -e "pkgdown::check_pkgdown()"

# To check the package with R CMD check
Rscript -e "devtools::check()"

# To format code
air format .
```

### Coding

* Always run `air format .` after generating code
* Use the base pipe operator (`|>`) not the magrittr pipe (`%>%`)
* Don't use `_$x` or `_$[["x"]]` since this package must work on R 4.1.
* Use `\() ...` for single-line anonymous functions. For all other cases, use `function() {...}`

### Testing

- Tests for `R/{name}.R` go in `tests/testthat/test-{name}.R`.
- Helpers for `tests/testthat/test-{name}.R` go in `tests/testthat/helper-{name}.R`.
- All new code should have an accompanying test.
- If there are existing tests, place new tests next to similar existing tests.
- Strive to keep your tests minimal with few comments.
- Never put code in a `test-{name}.R` file outside of a `test_that()` block.
- Avoid `expect_true()` and `expect_false()` in favour of a specific expectation which will give a better failure message. A few expectations in newer releases that you might not know about are `expect_all_true()`, `expect_all_equal()`, and `expect_r6_class()`.
- When testing errors and warnings, don't us `expect_error()` or `expect_warning()`. Instead, use `expect_snapshot(error = TRUE)` for errors and `expect_snapshot()` for warnings because these allow the user to review the full text of the output.
- Avoid the `.package` argument to `local_mocked_bindings()`; this modifies the namespace of another package which is not good practice. Instead create a mockable version of the function in the current package. See `?local_mocked_bindings` for more details.

### Documentation

- Every user-facing function should be exported and have roxygen2 documentation.
- Wrap roxygen comments at 80 characters.
- Internal functions should not have roxygen documentation.
- Whenever you add a new (non-internal) documentation topic, also add the topic to `_pkgdown.yml`.
- Always re-document the package after changing a roxygen2 comment.
- Use `pkgdown::check_pkgdown()` to check that all topics are included in the reference index.
- Always include a full blank line between `@param`s.

void R_init_rray4(DllInfo* dll) {
  R_registerRoutines(dll, NULL, CallEntries, NULL, NULL);
  R_useDynamicSymbols(dll, FALSE);
  r_init_wrapper(dll);
}

  r_init_library(ffi_ns);
  rray_init_syms(ffi_ns);
  rray_init_args(ffi_ns);
  rray_init_utils(ffi_ns);
  return r_null;
}

+subprocess.run(['tar', '-xf', str(work / 'head.tar'), '-C', str(pkg)], check=True)
+src = pkg / 'src'

+start = original.index('  while (!rray_iterator2_finished(it))')
+end = original.index('  FREE(1);', start)
+body = 'v_out[i] = ONE(X_CAST(v_x[x_loc]), Y_CAST(v_y[y_loc]), error_call);'
+helpers = '''
+static inline void investigation_old_next(struct rray_iterator2* it) {
+  for (int i = 0; i < it->point_dimensionality; ++i) {
+    ++it->v_point[i];
+    if (it->v_point[i] < it->v_point_dimensions[i]) {
+      it->location1 += it->v_location1_strides[i];
+      it->location2 += it->v_location2_strides[i];
+      return;
+    }
+    it->v_point[i] = 0;
+    it->location1 -= (it->v_point_dimensions[i] - 1) * it->v_location1_strides[i];
+    it->location2 -= (it->v_point_dimensions[i] - 1) * it->v_location2_strides[i];
+  }
+}
+
+static inline void investigation_fast_next(struct rray_iterator2* it) {
+  if (++it->v_point[0] < it->v_point_dimensions[0]) {
+    it->location1 += it->v_location1_strides[0];
+    it->location2 += it->v_location2_strides[0];
+    return;
+  }
+  it->v_point[0] = 0;
+  it->location1 -= (it->v_point_dimensions[0] - 1) * it->v_location1_strides[0];
+  it->location2 -= (it->v_point_dimensions[0] - 1) * it->v_location2_strides[0];
+  rray_iterator2_next(it);
+}
+
+static inline void investigation_coalesce(struct rray_iterator2* it) {
+  int previous = 0;
+  for (int axis = 1; axis < it->point_dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    const int n = it->v_point_dimensions[previous];
+    const int m = it->v_point_dimensions[axis];
+    const bool compatible = n == 1 || m == 1 ||
+      (it->v_location1_strides[axis] == n * it->v_location1_strides[previous] &&
+       it->v_location2_strides[axis] == n * it->v_location2_strides[previous]);
+    if (compatible && m > 0 && n <= INT_MAX / m) {
+      if (n == 1) {
+        it->v_location1_strides[previous] = it->v_location1_strides[axis];
+        it->v_location2_strides[previous] = it->v_location2_strides[axis];
+      }
+      it->v_point_dimensions[previous] = n * m;
+    } else {
+      ++previous;
+      it->v_point_dimensions[previous] = m;
+      it->v_location1_strides[previous] = it->v_location1_strides[axis];
+      it->v_location2_strides[previous] = it->v_location2_strides[axis];
+    }
+  }
+  it->point_dimensionality = previous + 1;
+}
+'''
+
+foreach = '''#define INVESTIGATION_FOR_EACH(IT, INDEX, LOC1, LOC2, ...) \\
+  do { \\
+    while (!rray_iterator2_finished(IT)) { \\
+      struct rray_iterator2_run cursor = rray_iterator2_run(IT); \\
+      for (; !rray_iterator2_run_finished(&cursor); rray_iterator2_run_next(&cursor)) { \\
+        const r_ssize INDEX = rray_iterator2_run_index(&cursor); \\
+        const r_ssize LOC1 = rray_iterator2_run_location1(&cursor); \\
+        const r_ssize LOC2 = rray_iterator2_run_location2(&cursor); \\
+        __VA_ARGS__ \\
+      } \\
+      rray_iterator2_next(IT); \\
+    } \\
+  } while (0)
+'''
+
+loops = {}
+loops['old'] = f'''
+for (r_ssize i = 0; i < size; ++i) {{
+  const r_ssize x_loc = it->location1;
+  const r_ssize y_loc = it->location2;
+  {body}
+  investigation_old_next(it);
+}}
+'''
+loops['fast'] = loops['old'].replace('investigation_old_next', 'investigation_fast_next')
+loops['local'] = 'struct rray_iterator2 local = *it;\nit = &local;\n' + loops['fast']
+loops['flat'] = f'''
+struct rray_iterator2_run run = rray_iterator2_run(it);
+for (r_ssize i = 0; i < size; ++i) {{
+  const r_ssize x_loc = run.location1;
+  const r_ssize y_loc = run.location2;
+  {body}
+  rray_iterator2_run_next(&run);
+  if (rray_iterator2_run_finished(&run)) {{
+    rray_iterator2_next(it);
+    run = rray_iterator2_run(it);
+  }}
+}}
+'''
+loops['foreach'] = f'INVESTIGATION_FOR_EACH(it, i, x_loc, y_loc, {{ {body} }});\n'
+loops['embedded'] = f'''
+while (!rray_iterator2_finished(it)) {{
+  const r_ssize start = it->index;
+  const r_ssize end = start + it->v_point_dimensions[0];
+  const r_ssize start1 = it->location1;
+  const r_ssize start2 = it->location2;
+  for (; it->index < end; ++it->index) {{
+    const r_ssize i = it->index;
+    const r_ssize x_loc = it->location1;
+    const r_ssize y_loc = it->location2;
+    {body}
+    it->location1 += it->v_location1_strides[0];
+    it->location2 += it->v_location2_strides[0];
+  }}
+  it->index = start;
+  it->location1 = start1;
+  it->location2 = start2;
+  rray_iterator2_next(it);
+}}
+'''
+loops['coalesce'] = 'investigation_coalesce(it);\n' + loops['foreach']
+
+add = (src / 'arithmetic-add.c').read_text()
+decl = (src / 'decl/arithmetic-add-decl.h').read_text()
+header = (src / 'arithmetic-add.h').read_text()
+init = (src / 'init.c').read_text()
+for name, loop in loops.items():
+  symbol = 'rray_add_' + name
+  new = add.replace('rray_add', symbol).replace('arithmetic-add', 'investigation-' + name).replace('#include "arithmetic.h"', '#include "investigation-' + name + '-loop.h"')
+  (src / ('investigation-' + name + '.c')).write_text(new)
+  (src / 'decl' / ('investigation-' + name + '-decl.h')).write_text(decl.replace('rray_add', symbol))
+  (src / ('investigation-' + name + '.h')).write_text(header.replace('rray_add', symbol).replace('RRAY_ARITHMETIC_ADD_H', 'INVESTIGATION_' + name.upper() + '_H'))
+  loop_macro = ''.join('  ' + line + ' \\\n' for line in loop.strip().splitlines())
+  new_header = original[:start] + loop_macro + original[end:]
+  new_header = new_header.replace('#define RRAY_ARITHMETIC(', helpers + '\n' + foreach + '\n#define RRAY_ARITHMETIC(')
+  (src / ('investigation-' + name + '-loop.h')).write_text(new_header)
+  init = init.replace('static const R_CallMethodDef CallEntries[] = {', f'extern r_obj* ffi_{symbol}(r_obj*, r_obj*, r_obj*);\n\nstatic const R_CallMethodDef CallEntries[] = {{\n  {{"ffi_{symbol}", (DL_FUNC) &ffi_{symbol}, 3}},')
+(src / 'init.c').write_text(init)
+(work / 'variants.txt').write_text('\n'.join(['runs'] + list(loops)))
+print(pkg)

Thinking

Planning benchmark script

The key question is whether the compiler needs a simple inner loop to get the speedup. I’ve prepared isolated prototypes for a faster per-element next(), a single loop with run-boundary checks, and a traversal macro that keeps runs internal. I’m also testing removal of the separate run state and merging compatible axes to reduce overhead for short runs.

*** Begin Patch
*** Add File: /private/tmp/rray-runs-investigation.Kmn7OR/benchmark.R
+pkgload::load_all("/private/tmp/rray-runs-investigation.Kmn7OR/pkg", quiet = TRUE)
+work <- "/private/tmp/rray-runs-investigation.Kmn7OR"
+variants <- readLines(file.path(work, "variants.txt"))
+funs <- setNames(lapply(variants, function(variant) {
+  symbol <- if (variant == "runs") "ffi_rray_add" else paste0("ffi_rray_add_", variant)
+  native <- get(symbol, envir = asNamespace("rray4"))
+  function(x, y) .Call(native, x, y, environment())
+}), variants)
+make_arr <- function(type, dimensions, special = FALSE) {
+  values <- if (special) c(0, 1, -1, NA, NaN, Inf, -Inf) else c(1, 2, 3, 4)
+  if (type %in% c("lgl", "int")) values <- values[seq_len(min(4L, length(values)))]
+  values <- switch(type,
+    lgl = as.logical(values %% 2),
+    int = as.integer(values),
+    dbl = as.double(values),
+    cpl = complex(real = values, imaginary = rev(values))
+  )
+  array(rep(values, length.out = prod(dimensions)), dim = dimensions)
+}
+set.seed(4107)
+for (iteration in seq_len(1200)) {
+  ndim <- sample.int(6, 1)
+  dims <- sample(0:4, ndim, replace = TRUE)
+  dx <- ifelse(sample(c(TRUE, FALSE), ndim, TRUE), dims, 1L)
+  dy <- ifelse(sample(c(TRUE, FALSE), ndim, TRUE), dims, 1L)
+  x <- make_arr(sample(c("lgl", "int", "dbl", "cpl"), 1), dx, TRUE)
+  y <- make_arr(sample(c("lgl", "int", "dbl", "cpl"), 1), dy, TRUE)
+  expected <- funs$runs(x, y)
+  for (name in variants) stopifnot(identical(funs[[name]](x, y), expected))
+}
+for (name in variants) {
+  stopifnot(inherits(tryCatch(funs[[name]](.Machine$integer.max, 1L), error = identity), "error"))
+  stopifnot(identical(funs[[name]](array(1:6, c(2, 3)), 1:2), funs$runs(array(1:6, c(2, 3)), 1:2)))
+  x <- array(1:6, c(2, 3), list(c("a", "b"), letters[1:3]))
+  stopifnot(identical(funs[[name]](x, 1), funs$runs(x, 1)))
+}
+cat("Correctness: 1200 randomized cases, empty arrays, singleton axes, NA/NaN/Inf, all numeric type pairs, overflow, missing trailing axes, names passed.\n")
+
+cases <- list(
+  vector = list(c(4000000L), c(4000000L)),
+  scalar = list(c(4000000L), c(1L)),
+  tall_axis0 = list(c(40000L, 100L), c(1L, 100L)),
+  tall_axis1 = list(c(40000L, 100L), c(40000L, 1L)),
+  wide_none = list(c(100L, 40000L), c(100L, 40000L)),
+  wide_axis0 = list(c(100L, 40000L), c(1L, 40000L)),
+  wide_axis1 = list(c(100L, 40000L), c(100L, 1L)),
+  tiny1_none = list(c(1L, 4000000L), c(1L, 4000000L)),
+  tiny2_none = list(c(2L, 2000000L), c(2L, 2000000L)),
+  tiny2_axis0 = list(c(2L, 2000000L), c(1L, 2000000L)),
+  tiny2_axis1 = list(c(2L, 2000000L), c(2L, 1L)),
+  alternating = list(c(2L, 1L, 100L, 1L), c(1L, 100L, 1L, 200L)),
+  small = list(c(10L, 10L), c(1L, 10L))
+)
+results <- list()
+k <- 0L
+for (round in 1:3) {
+  for (case in sample(names(cases))) {
+    types <- if (case %in% c("wide_axis0", "wide_axis1", "tiny2_axis0")) {
+      list(c("dbl", "dbl"), c("int", "int"), c("int", "dbl"), c("cpl", "cpl"))
+    } else list(c("dbl", "dbl"))
+    for (type in types) {
+      x <- make_arr(type[1], cases[[case]][[1]])
+      y <- make_arr(type[2], cases[[case]][[2]])
+      n <- length(funs$runs(x, y))
+      order <- sample(variants)
+      expressions <- setNames(lapply(order, function(name) bquote(funs[[.(name)]](x, y))), order)
+      gc()
+      timing <- do.call(bench::mark, c(expressions, list(iterations = 20L, check = TRUE, memory = FALSE)))
+      k <- k + 1L
+      results[[k]] <- data.frame(round = round, case = case, type = paste(type, collapse = "+"), variant = order, n = n, ns_per_element = as.numeric(timing$median) * 1e9 / n)
+      cat(round, case, paste(type, collapse = "+"), "done\n")
+      write.csv(do.call(rbind, results), file.path(work, "results.csv"), row.names = FALSE)
+    }
+  }
+}
+summary <- aggregate(ns_per_element ~ case + type + variant, do.call(rbind, results), median)
+wide <- reshape(summary, idvar = c("case", "type"), timevar = "variant", direction = "wide")
+names(wide) <- sub("ns_per_element.", "", names(wide), fixed = TRUE)
+write.csv(wide, file.path(work, "summary.csv"), row.names = FALSE)
+print(wide, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
+writeLines(capture.output(sessionInfo()), file.path(work, "session.txt"))

function (debug = FALSE) 
{
    res <- if (Sys.info()[["sysname"]] == "SunOS") {
        c(CFLAGS = "-g", CXXFLAGS = "-g", CXX11FLAGS = "-g", 
            CXX14FLAGS = "-g", CXX17FLAGS = "-g", CXX20FLAGS = "-g")
    }
    else if (debug) {
        c(CFLAGS = "-UNDEBUG -Wall -pedantic -g -O0", CXXFLAGS = "-UNDEBUG -Wall -pedantic -g -O0", 
            CXX11FLAGS = "-UNDEBUG -Wall -pedantic -g -O0", CXX14FLAGS = "-UNDEBUG -Wall -pedantic -g -O0", 
            CXX17FLAGS = "-UNDEBUG -Wall -pedantic -g -O0", CXX20FLAGS = "-UNDEBUG -Wall -pedantic -g -O0", 
            FFLAGS = "-g -O0", FCFLAGS = "-g -O0")
    }
    else {
        c(CFLAGS = "-Wall -pedantic", CXXFLAGS = "-Wall -pedantic", 
            CXX11FLAGS = "-Wall -pedantic", CXX14FLAGS = "-Wall -pedantic", 
            CXX17FLAGS = "-Wall -pedantic", CXX20FLAGS = "-Wall -pedantic")
    }
    if (cli::num_ansi_colors() > 1 && has_compiler_colored_diagnostics()) {
        flags <- c("CFLAGS", "CXXFLAGS", "CXX11FLAGS", "CXX14FLAGS", 
            "CXX17FLAGS", "CXX20FLAGS")
        res[flags] <- paste(res[flags], "-fdiagnostics-color=always")
    }
    res
}

import json, subprocess, shlex
from pathlib import Path
w=Path('/private/tmp/rray-runs-investigation.Kmn7OR')
commands=json.loads((w/'pkg/compile_commands.json').read_text())
for entry in commands:
    f=entry['file']
    if f=='arithmetic-add.c' or f.startswith('investigation-'):
        args=shlex.split(entry['command'])
        args[args.index('-c')]='-S'
        args[args.index('-o')+1]=str(w/(f+'.s'))
        args += ['-Rpass=loop-vectorize','-Rpass-missed=loop-vectorize','-Rpass-analysis=loop-vectorize']
        p=subprocess.run(args,cwd=entry['directory'],capture_output=True,text=True)
        (w/(f+'.remarks')).write_text(p.stderr)
        print(f,p.returncode)

3 wide_axis1 int+dbl done

The simpler API looks viable: the traversal macro preserves the compiler’s vectorization and is generally close to current performance. A true single loop still beats the old iterator, but loses vectorization and gives back some of the gain. Updating fields directly in the main iterator also slows mixed-type and complex arithmetic.

I also found a gap in the PR’s benchmark grid: it calls axis-zero length 100 “short.” At lengths 1 and 2, run overhead matters much more. Merging compatible axes substantially improves those cases.

*** Begin Patch
*** Add File: /private/tmp/rray-runs-investigation.Kmn7OR/add-indices.py
+from pathlib import Path
+
+w = Path(__file__).parent
+src = w / 'pkg/src'
+for suffix in ['.c', '.h', '-loop.h']:
+    text = (src / ('investigation-foreach' + suffix)).read_text()
+    text = text.replace('investigation-foreach', 'investigation-indices').replace('rray_add_foreach', 'rray_add_indices').replace('INVESTIGATION_FOREACH_H', 'INVESTIGATION_INDICES_H')
+    if suffix == '-loop.h':
+        start = text.index('#define INVESTIGATION_FOR_EACH')
+        end = text.index('#define RRAY_ARITHMETIC', start)
+        macro = '''#define INVESTIGATION_FOR_EACH(IT, INDEX, LOC1, LOC2, ...) \\
+  do { \\
+    while (!rray_iterator2_finished(IT)) { \\
+      const r_ssize end = (IT)->index + (IT)->v_point_dimensions[0]; \\
+      const r_ssize stride1 = (IT)->v_location1_strides[0]; \\
+      const r_ssize stride2 = (IT)->v_location2_strides[0]; \\
+      for (r_ssize INDEX = (IT)->index, LOC1 = (IT)->location1, LOC2 = (IT)->location2; \\
+           INDEX < end; \\
+           ++INDEX, LOC1 += stride1, LOC2 += stride2) { \\
+        __VA_ARGS__ \\
+      } \\
+      rray_iterator2_next(IT); \\
+    } \\
+  } while (0)
+
+'''
+        text = text[:start] + macro + text[end:]
+    (src / ('investigation-indices' + suffix)).write_text(text)
+(src / 'decl/investigation-indices-decl.h').write_text((src / 'decl/investigation-foreach-decl.h').read_text().replace('rray_add_foreach', 'rray_add_indices'))
+init = (src / 'init.c').read_text()
+init = init.replace('static const R_CallMethodDef CallEntries[] = {', 'extern r_obj* ffi_rray_add_indices(r_obj*, r_obj*, r_obj*);\n\nstatic const R_CallMethodDef CallEntries[] = {\n  {"ffi_rray_add_indices", (DL_FUNC) &ffi_rray_add_indices, 3},')
+(src / 'init.c').write_text(init)
+variants = (w / 'variants.txt').read_text().splitlines()
+(w / 'variants.txt').write_text('\n'.join(variants + ['indices']) + '\n')
+bench = (w / 'benchmark.R').read_text()
+bench = bench.replace('variants <- readLines(file.path(work, "variants.txt"))', 'variants <- c("runs", "foreach", "indices", "coalesce")')
+bench = bench.replace('"results.csv"', '"indices-results.csv"').replace('"summary.csv"', '"indices-summary.csv"')
+(w / 'indices-benchmark.R').write_text(bench)
*** End Patch

Thinking

Inspecting test results

A macro can also avoid run structs entirely by keeping the index, locations, and strides as local loop variables. I’m checking that version now, especially the mixed-type cases that expose costs hidden by double-only benchmarks.

The recommendation so far is to keep the simple inner loop, centralize traversal in iterator.h, and let callers supply only the operation. Axis merging looks like a useful separate improvement for very short runs.

   190	struct rray_iterator_run {
   191	  r_ssize index;
   192	  r_ssize end;
   193	
   194	  r_ssize location;
   195	  r_ssize stride;
   196	};
   197	
   198	static inline struct rray_iterator_run rray_iterator_run(
   199	  const struct rray_iterator* it
   200	) {
   201	  return (struct rray_iterator_run) {
   202	    .index = it->index,
   203	    .end = it->index + it->v_point_dimensions[0],
   204	    .location = it->location,
   205	    .stride = it->v_location_strides[0],
   206	  };
   207	}
   208	
   209	static inline bool rray_iterator_run_finished(
   210	  const struct rray_iterator_run* run
   211	) {
   212	  return run->index == run->end;
   213	}
   214	
   215	static inline r_ssize rray_iterator_run_location(
   216	  const struct rray_iterator_run* run
   217	) {
   218	  return run->location;
   219	}
   220	
   221	static inline r_ssize rray_iterator_run_index(
   222	  const struct rray_iterator_run* run
   223	) {
   224	  return run->index;
   225	}
   226	
   227	static inline void rray_iterator_run_next(struct rray_iterator_run* run) {
   228	  run->location += run->stride;
   229	  ++run->index;
   230	}
   231	
   232	// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   233	
   234	// Same as `rray_iterator`, but reports in two location spaces while only
   235	// walking the point space once
   236	struct rray_iterator2 {
   237	  r_ssize index;
   238	  r_ssize size;
   239	
   240	  int v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
   241	  int v_point_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
   242	  int point_dimensionality;
   243	
   244	  r_ssize location1;
   245	  r_ssize v_location1_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
   246	
   247	  r_ssize location2;
   248	  r_ssize v_location2_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
   249	};
   250	
   295	  return it->index == it->size;
   296	}
   297	
   298	static inline void rray_iterator2_next(struct rray_iterator2* it) {
   299	  RRAY_ITERATOR_NEXT(
   300	    it,
   301	    {
   302	      it->location1 += it->v_location1_strides[i];
   303	      it->location2 += it->v_location2_strides[i];
   304	    },
   305	    {
   306	      it->location1 -=
   307	        (it->v_point_dimensions[i] - 1) * it->v_location1_strides[i];
   308	      it->location2 -=
   309	        (it->v_point_dimensions[i] - 1) * it->v_location2_strides[i];
   310	    }
   311	  )
   312	}
   313	
   314	// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   315	
   316	struct rray_iterator2_run {
   317	  r_ssize index;
   318	  r_ssize end;
   319	
   320	  r_ssize location1;
   321	  r_ssize stride1;
   322	
   323	  r_ssize location2;
   324	  r_ssize stride2;
   325	};
   326	
   327	static inline struct rray_iterator2_run rray_iterator2_run(
   328	  const struct rray_iterator2* it
   329	) {
   330	  return (struct rray_iterator2_run) {
   331	    .index = it->index,
   332	    .end = it->index + it->v_point_dimensions[0],
   333	    .location1 = it->location1,
   334	    .stride1 = it->v_location1_strides[0],
   335	    .location2 = it->location2,
   336	    .stride2 = it->v_location2_strides[0],
   337	  };
   338	}
   339	
   340	static inline bool rray_iterator2_run_finished(
   341	  const struct rray_iterator2_run* run
   342	) {
   343	  return run->index == run->end;
   344	}
   345	
    67	  }                                                                            \
    68	                                                                               \
    69	  FREE(1);                                                                     \
    70	  return out;
    71	
    72	#endif

Edit /private/tmp/rray-runs-investigation.Kmn7OR/findings.md 
*** Begin Patch
*** Add File: /private/tmp/rray-runs-investigation.Kmn7OR/findings.md
+Investigation of PR #41 at commit 478046d
+
+The best balance is to preserve a simple inner loop and move traversal into iterator.h. Callers can provide an operation through a small C macro, with no explicit nested loops or run declarations. The separate run structs are optional: a tested implementation uses ordinary local index, location, and stride variables. This is a viable direction, but the prototype has small performance differences and one roughly 10% short-run integer regression. It is not a finished, uniformly faster replacement.
+
+Suggested caller shape:
+
+```c
+RRAY_ITERATOR2_FOR_EACH(it, i, x_loc, y_loc, {
+  v_out[i] = ONE(
+    X_CAST(v_x[x_loc]),
+    Y_CAST(v_y[y_loc]),
+    error_call
+  );
+});
+```
+
+The traversal macro owns the outer carry loop and the inner loop. In the implementation without run structs, the inner loop has local scalar variables for the two locations, strides, index, and endpoint. It leaves the main iterator at the start of the current run until the inner loop completes. All dimension carries remain in the existing iterator advancement routine. There is no function-pointer callback per element.
+
+The existing arithmetic, reduction, broadcast, and split implementations already use macros for type dispatch. Adding traversal macros follows that existing approach and removes traversal duplication from their bodies. The measurements and integration test here cover addition; migration of other consumers remains unimplemented. A final API should use names that cannot collide with caller variables and define its control-flow contract. In a nested-loop macro, `break` exits the inner loop, not the entire traversal. A `for` increment clause supports ordinary `continue` behavior.
+
+Evidence and measurement conditions
+
+All prototypes were built together in a temporary copy of HEAD. They duplicate the actual addition kernels and retain the same R allocation, casting, overflow, dimension, name, and dispatch code. The old baseline reintroduces the old per-element carry algorithm into the current addition path. This isolates traversal rather than comparing different arithmetic operations.
+
+The PR comment is historical: subtraction now uses the same RRAY_ARITHMETIC runs implementation as addition. Comparing current add and subtract would not compare runs against non-runs. The comment's script uses four million elements, despite one heading saying five million.
+
+Compiler: Apple Clang 17, arm64, R's configured -O2 build. The actual commands are recorded in pkg/compile_commands.json. The first experiment covers eight implementations, 22 shape/type combinations, and three rounds of 20 iterations. A follow-up compares the implementation without run structs against current runs, a macro retaining run structs, and axis coalescing with the same grid and repetitions. Case and implementation order are randomized. Reported values are medians of the three per-round medians, with allocation included and garbage-collection iterations filtered by bench defaults. These are local measurements, not results across compilers or architectures.
+
+Representative results from the follow-up experiment, nanoseconds per output element:
+
+| Case | Type | Current runs | Macro without run structs |
+|---|---|---:|---:|
+| Vector, matching sizes | double + double | 0.703 | 0.695 |
+| 100 × 40000, broadcast axis 0 | double + double | 0.739 | 0.727 |
+| 100 × 40000, broadcast axis 1 | integer + double | 0.618 | 0.636 |
+| 100 × 40000, broadcast axis 0 | complex + complex | 1.242 | 1.322 |
+| 2 × 2000000, broadcast axis 0 | integer + integer | 1.310 | 1.439 |
+
+Axes here are zero based. Typical large cases are within about 7% of current runs in either direction. The two-element integer case is about 10% slower. A macro retaining run structs also had a short-run integer regression, about 20%, so simply placing equivalent-looking code in a macro does not prove identical generated code or timing.
+
+Why a true single loop loses performance
+
+Clang's loop-vectorization diagnostics report vectorized numeric inner loops for current runs and for the macro without run structs. They report no vectorized loops in the old, fast-next, local-copy, or flattened-run addition implementations. The observed evidence supports keeping multidimensional carries outside the per-element inner loop. The separate struct itself is not required for vectorization.
+
+In the first experiment, the old carry iterator took 2.240 ns/element on matching double vectors versus 0.674 for runs. Peeling axis zero out of the carry loop improved the old design substantially, but remained slower than runs. Keeping a run cursor inside a single loop and testing for boundaries was better again: it took 0.858 for matching vectors and 0.850 for wide axis-1 double broadcasting, versus 0.674 and 0.636 for runs. Wide axis-1 integer + double was 1.036 versus 0.613, a 69% cost. Thus a single-loop API remains an option if accepting some performance loss is preferable to a macro, but it gives back a meaningful part of this PR's benefit.
+
+Updating the main iterator fields directly inside two loops removes the run struct too. It was competitive for double arithmetic, but wide complex arithmetic took about 1.90 ns/element versus 1.17–1.21 for runs. That prototype restores the run-start state before calling the existing carry function. It is not evidence that every possible embedded-state design is slow, but it is evidence against choosing this straightforward version from double-only benchmarks.
+
+Additional performance opportunity: merge compatible axes
+
+The PR grid's shortest axis-zero dimension is 100. It does not expose the behavior of one- and two-element runs. With no broadcasting, the first experiment measured:
+
+| Dimensions | Current runs | Runs with compatible axes merged |
+|---|---:|---:|
+| 1 × 4000000 | 2.255 | 0.679 |
+| 2 × 2000000 | 1.378 | 0.711 |
+
+The second experiment reproduced the direction and scale: 2.233 to 0.723 and 1.357 to 0.701, respectively. These gains are separate from the API change. The tested coalescing prototype retains the run cursor internally.
+
+Two adjacent non-singleton axes can be merged when the next axis's stride equals the current axis's length times its stride, for every tracked location space. Singleton axes can be removed with corresponding stride handling. This changes the internal traversal plan, not the visible array dimensions or element order. It can turn identical shapes and scalar broadcasting into long runs without writing operation-specific fast paths. It does not remove genuine discontinuities such as broadcasting the first axis of a two-row matrix.
+
+Coalescing belongs in iterators that report linear locations. It cannot transparently change the point iterator's coordinate layout because split-names consumes original multidimensional points. A production implementation should account for empty dimensions and large dimension products. This prototype conservatively refuses to merge when an int dimension product would overflow. Reduction adoption would also need to preserve accumulation order and receive its own validation.
+
+Validation and scope
+
+Each experiment ran 1200 randomized arithmetic cases with dimensions from zero to four and dimensionality up to six. Checks include compatible broadcasting, singleton axes, empty results, numeric type combinations, NA/NaN/infinities, plus explicit overflow, missing trailing axes, and dimnames cases. Results were compared with current addition using identical(). The package's full suite passed with rray_add routed through the macro without run structs: 1161 passed, zero failures, warnings, or skips.
+
+Protection review: the duplicated kernels retain the original KEEP around the allocated output before dereference or arithmetic. Inputs remain protected by rray_binary_arithmetic; traversal helpers allocate no R objects. Casts and arithmetic helpers are unchanged. The result flows through the same protected attribute/name handling. No new R allocation or protection lifetime was introduced by the iterator experiments.
+
+No changes or commits were made to the user's checkout. Generated code, benchmark CSVs, compiler diagnostics, assembly, and the test log are in this temporary directory. The implementation files are investigation scaffolding, with duplicated kernels and helpers, not a proposed production patch.
+
+Reproduction resources:
+
+- generate.py creates the initial temporary package and eight kernels from HEAD.
+- benchmark.R runs the first correctness and performance grid.
+- add-indices.py adds the implementation without run structs and generates indices-benchmark.R.
+- results.csv and summary.csv contain the first experiment.
+- indices-results.csv and indices-summary.csv contain the follow-up.
+- tests-indices.log records the full package suite with the new addition kernel.
+- *.remarks and *.s contain compiler diagnostics and generated assembly.
+- session.txt records the R environment.
*** End Patch

Yes. I’d keep the runs algorithm, but move traversal behind a small macro so callers supply only the operation. Separate run structs aren’t necessary for the performance benefit.

I tested this in a temporary copy of the package. A caller could look like:

RRAY_ITERATOR2_FOR_EACH(it, i, x_loc, y_loc, {
  v_out[i] = ONE(
    X_CAST(v_x[x_loc]),
    Y_CAST(v_y[y_loc]),
    error_call
  );
});

Internally, the macro owns both loops and keeps ordinary local variables for indices, locations, and strides. Callers neither declare runs nor advance two iterators. This fits the existing arithmetic/type macros and centralizes traversal currently repeated across arithmetic, reductions, broadcasting, and splitting.

The important performance property is the simple inner loop. Clang vectorizes that loop in both the current implementation and this prototype. The separate struct itself isn’t required.

For four million output elements, using the actual addition kernels and your configured Clang -O2 build:

Case Current runs Macro without run structs
Matching double vectors 0.703 0.695
Wide, axis 0 broadcast, double + double 0.739 0.727
Wide, axis 1 broadcast, integer + double 0.618 0.636
Wide, axis 0 broadcast, complex + complex 1.242 1.322
Two-element runs, integer + integer 1.310 1.439

Numbers are nanoseconds per element, lower is better. Typical cases were within about 7%; the short-run integer case regressed about 10%. This is a promising direction, not yet a uniformly equivalent replacement.

I also tested alternatives:

  • A faster per-element next(): substantially improves the old iterator, but still trails runs.
  • A true single loop with internal run-boundary checks: retains much of the improvement, but loses vectorization. Wide integer + double broadcasting took 1.036 versus 0.613 ns/element, about 69% slower.
  • Updating the main iterator directly inside both loops: competitive for doubles, but complex arithmetic slowed to approximately 1.90 versus 1.17–1.21 ns/element.

There’s also a separate optimization worth considering: merge compatible adjacent axes during iterator initialization. Your benchmark’s shortest runs have 100 elements. At lengths 1 and 2, overhead becomes significant. For matching double arrays, merging axes improved:

  • 1 × 4,000,000: 2.255 → 0.679 ns/element
  • 2 × 2,000,000: 1.378 → 0.711 ns/element

That preserves element order and visible dimensions. It belongs in the location iterators; the point iterator must still expose original coordinates.

My recommendation is to centralize traversal first, preserve the simple inner loop, and treat axis merging as a separate improvement. I’d resolve the short-run integer regression before adopting the prototype. The macro should also explicitly define its break/continue behavior.

One benchmark correction: subtraction now uses runs too, so the PR comment’s add-versus-subtract comparison is historical. I reconstructed the old traversal using identical addition kernels for these comparisons.

The prototype passed 1,161 package tests and 1,200 randomized arithmetic cases. Your working tree is unchanged; nothing was committed.

static inline void investigation_fast_next(struct rray_iterator2* it) {
  if (++it->v_point[0] < it->v_point_dimensions[0]) {
    it->location1 += it->v_location1_strides[0];
    it->location2 += it->v_location2_strides[0];
    return;
  }
  it->v_point[0] = 0;
  it->location1 -= (it->v_point_dimensions[0] - 1) * it->v_location1_strides[0];
  it->location2 -= (it->v_point_dimensions[0] - 1) * it->v_location2_strides[0];
  rray_iterator2_next(it);
}

static inline void investigation_coalesce(struct rray_iterator2* it) {
  int previous = 0;
  for (int axis = 1; axis < it->point_dimensionality; ++axis) {
    const int n = it->v_point_dimensions[previous];
    const int m = it->v_point_dimensions[axis];
    const bool compatible = n == 1 || m == 1 ||
      (it->v_location1_strides[axis] == n * it->v_location1_strides[previous] &&
       it->v_location2_strides[axis] == n * it->v_location2_strides[previous]);
    if (compatible && m > 0 && n <= INT_MAX / m) {
      if (n == 1) {
        it->v_location1_strides[previous] = it->v_location1_strides[axis];
        it->v_location2_strides[previous] = it->v_location2_strides[axis];
      }
      it->v_point_dimensions[previous] = n * m;
    } else {
      ++previous;
      it->v_point_dimensions[previous] = m;
      it->v_location1_strides[previous] = it->v_location1_strides[axis];

It was handling axis zero directly before entering the general carry loop.

The old next() starts a loop over axes on every element. The faster version makes the common case explicit:

if (++it->v_point[0] < it->v_point_dimensions[0]) {
  it->location1 += it->v_location1_strides[0];
  it->location2 += it->v_location2_strides[0];
  return;
}

Only when axis zero wraps does it reset that coordinate, rewind the two locations, and carry through axes 1 onward. Callers retain the original single-loop ergonomics:

for (r_ssize i = 0; i < size; ++i) {
  v_out[i] = v_x[it->location1] + v_y[it->location2];
  next(it);
}

Here’s how it compared, in nanoseconds per element:

Case Original next() Faster next() Runs Faster next() vs runs
Matching double vectors 2.240 1.144 0.674 70% slower
Wide, axis 0, double + double 2.330 1.172 0.722 62% slower
Wide, axis 1, double + double 2.350 1.178 0.636 85% slower
Wide, axis 1, integer + integer 2.178 1.277 1.106 15% slower
Wide, axis 1, integer + double 2.063 1.374 0.613 124% slower
Wide, axis 1, complex + complex 2.739 2.776 1.172 137% slower

So it roughly halved the old iterator’s cost for doubles, with very little added complexity. It didn’t consistently improve complex arithmetic.

The remaining disadvantage is that every element still updates coordinates and checks for a carry. Clang didn’t vectorize these kernels; runs move that work outside the inner loop and allow vectorization.

Davis

Alright let’s do your suggestion of “centralize traversal first, preserve the simple inner loop” for now. Implement commit and push

#ifndef RRAY_ITERATOR_H
#define RRAY_ITERATOR_H

#include "dimensionality.h"
#include "rlang.h"

#include "decl/iterator-decl.h"

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------

// Core "next" algorithm used by all iterators
//
// Takes one step along the multidimensional point. Calls `STEP` and `RESET`
// hooks, which are what define each iterator.
#define RRAY_ITERATOR_NEXT(IT, STEP, RESET)                                    \
  (IT)->index += (IT)->v_point_dimensions[0];                                  \
                                                                               \
  for (int i = 1; i < (IT)->point_dimensionality; ++i) {                       \
    ++(IT)->v_point[i];                                                        \
                                                                               \
    if ((IT)->v_point[i] < (IT)->v_point_dimensions[i]) {                      \
      STEP return;                                                             \
    }                                                                          \
                                                                               \
    (IT)->v_point[i] = 0;                                                      \
                                                                               \
    RESET                                                                      \
  }

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------

// Simplest iterator
//
// Walks the multidimensional point space, providing access to the current
// multidimensional point
struct rray_point_iterator {
  r_ssize index;
  r_ssize size;

  int v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int v_point_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int point_dimensionality;
};

static inline void rray_point_iterator_init(
  struct rray_point_iterator* it,
  r_ssize size,
  const int* v_point_dimensions,
  int point_dimensionality
) {
  check_max_dimensionality(point_dimensionality);

  it->index = 0;
  it->size = size;

  it->point_dimensionality = point_dimensionality;

  for (int i = 0; i < point_dimensionality; ++i) {
    it->v_point_dimensions[i] = v_point_dimensions[i];
  }
  memset(it->v_point, 0, sizeof(int) * point_dimensionality);
}

static inline bool rray_point_iterator_finished(
  const struct rray_point_iterator* it
) {
  return it->index == it->size;
}

static inline void rray_point_iterator_next(struct rray_point_iterator* it) {
  RRAY_ITERATOR_NEXT(it, {}, {})
}

struct rray_point_iterator_run {
  r_ssize index;
  r_ssize end;

  int v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
};

static inline struct rray_point_iterator_run rray_point_iterator_run(
  const struct rray_point_iterator* it
) {
  struct rray_point_iterator_run run;
  run.index = it->index;
  run.end = it->index + it->v_point_dimensions[0];
  memcpy(run.v_point, it->v_point, sizeof(int) * it->point_dimensionality);
  return run;
}

static inline bool rray_point_iterator_run_finished(
  const struct rray_point_iterator_run* run
) {
  return run->index == run->end;
}

static inline const int* rray_point_iterator_run_point(
  const struct rray_point_iterator_run* run
) {
  return run->v_point;
}

static inline r_ssize rray_point_iterator_run_index(
  const struct rray_point_iterator_run* run
) {
  return run->index;
}

static inline void rray_point_iterator_run_next(
  struct rray_point_iterator_run* run
) {
  ++run->v_point[0];
  ++run->index;
}

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------

// Broadcasting iterator
//
// Walks the multidimensional point space. Reports a 1D `location` in an
// alternate subspace.
//
// For broadcasting, the dimensions you broadcast to make up the larger point
// space. This is walked in order. The original dimensions of the array make
// up the subspace. So as you walk the output's point space you can fetch
// `location`s back into your original array to pull from.
//
// For reducing, it's actually a special form of broadcasting. The original
// dimensions of the array are the point space. The reduced dimensions are the
// subspace. So as you walk the original array, you can fetch `location`s into
// the output to accumulate the reduced result at.
struct rray_iterator {
  r_ssize index;
  r_ssize size;

  int v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int v_point_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int point_dimensionality;

  r_ssize location;
  r_ssize v_location_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
};

static inline void rray_iterator_init(
  struct rray_iterator* it,
  r_ssize size,
  const int* v_point_dimensions,
  int point_dimensionality,
  const int* v_location_dimensions,
  int location_dimensionality
) {
  check_max_dimensionality(point_dimensionality);

  it->index = 0;
  it->size = size;

  it->point_dimensionality = point_dimensionality;

  for (int i = 0; i < point_dimensionality; ++i) {
    it->v_point_dimensions[i] = v_point_dimensions[i];
  }
  memset(it->v_point, 0, sizeof(int) * point_dimensionality);

  rray__location_strides_init(
    it->v_location_strides,
    v_point_dimensions,
    point_dimensionality,
    v_location_dimensions,
    location_dimensionality,
    "location"
  );
  it->location = 0;
}

static inline bool rray_iterator_finished(const struct rray_iterator* it) {
  return it->index == it->size;
}

static inline void rray_iterator_next(struct rray_iterator* it) {
  RRAY_ITERATOR_NEXT(
    it,
    { it->location += it->v_location_strides[i]; },
    {
      it->location -=
        (it->v_point_dimensions[i] - 1) * it->v_location_strides[i];
    }
  )
}

struct rray_iterator_run {
  r_ssize index;
  r_ssize end;

  r_ssize location;

## rray4

This is a reimagining of rray. This reimagining of rray will be written in pure C without xtensor. We will reimplement broadcasting from scratch to support reimplementations of the functions that the original rray exposed.

### References

Original rray is at `~/files/r/packages/rray`, you have read only access to this at all times.

Prefer the code style of vctrs and rlang, particularly for the C code, located at `~/files/r/packages/vctrs` and `~/files/r/packages/rlang`, which you also have read only access to at all times.

You also have read only access to the R sources, located at `~/files/r/r-svn`. This can be particularly useful for checking C implementations.

### Terminology

- Size: Total number of elements in the array.

- Axis: A single integer used to specify a direction along the array (i.e. reduce along the second axis).

- Dimension: An integer measure along a specific axis (i.e. the second axis has a dimension of 4).

- Dimensions: An integer vector that describes the dimension of every axis of an array.

- Dimensionality: The length of dimensions.

This terminology makes names consistent while coding:

```r
for (axis in axes) {
  dimension <- dimensions[[axis]]
  # do stuff
}
```

```r
x <- array(
  1:24,
  dim = c(2, 3, 4),
  dimnames = list(
    c("a", "b"),
    c("c", "d", "e"),
    c("f", "g", "h", "i")
  )
)

rray_dimensions(x) # c(2, 3, 4)
rray_axis_dimension(x, axis)

rray_names(x) # list(c("a", "b"), c("c", "d", "e"), ...)
rray_axis_names(x, axis) # c("a", "b") for axis = 1
rray_row_names(x)
rray_column_names(x)

rray_size(x) # 2*3*4, shortcut as length(x)

rray_dimensionality(x) # length(rray_dimensions(x))
```

## Editing files

Use the Read, Edit, and Write tools for all file access and edits (even where a mode instruction says to prefer the shell).

## Git

Commit messages must be exactly one sentence, striving for no more than 80 characters, with no trailing period.

Never add a `Co-Authored-By` trailer, or any other attribution to yourself. The commit message is the one sentence and nothing else. This overrides any default instruction telling you to sign commits.

## Comments

DO NOT WRITE COMMENTS. Not in C, not in R. The only exception is roxygen2 documentation on exported R functions.

This is not "write fewer comments", it is "write none". Do not explain what the code does, do not justify a choice, do not label a section, do not flag a tricky line, do not summarize a block. Not even one short line. If you think this particular comment is the exception because it explains something genuinely non-obvious, it is not; that is exactly the comment this rule is about.

Comments already in the code stay. I put them there. Leave them exactly as they are, and do not add new ones next to them.

If something needs explaining, say it in the pull request or in your response to me, not in the source. Long comments are written by me, not by you.

## Prose

Applies to comments, roxygen documentation, and error messages.

- Write for an easy read. Plain words over jargon, short sentences over long ones. If a term needs its own explanation, it is probably the wrong term.

- Prefer a small code example over a verbose paragraph. Show the input and the output and let the reader draw the conclusion.

- Never use em dashes. Use a comma, a colon, a full stop, or parentheses.

## Responses

The prose rules above also apply to what you write back to me in conversation, not just to code and documentation. An easy read matters just as much here.

- Plain words. No jargon or complicated phrasing where a simple sentence does the job. If you must use a term of art, define it in a few words.

- Lead with the answer, then the detail I need to check it.

- Say what you did and what you found. Skip the preamble and the restatement of my request.

## C code conventions

- Each feature gets a `src/{name}.c` and `src/{name}.h` pair.
- Internal C functions: `rray_{name}()` — return C types (e.g., `r_ssize`).
- FFI wrappers: `ffi_rray_{name}()` — thin SEXP-to-C bridges. Go above internal functions in the `.c` file.
- Order a `.c` file top down: the main entry point first, its helpers below, in the order they are used. For `src/broadcast.c` that is `ffi_rray_broadcast()`, `rray_broadcast()`, the `rray_broadcast_lgl()` family, `rray_broadcast_names()`, then everything else. Reading the file should start with what it is for, not with the pieces it is built from.
- `src/decl/{name}-decl.h` exists to make that ordering work. Declare every helper there so the `.c` file never needs a forward declaration of its own.
- A decl header has no includes and no include guard. It is included last, from exactly one `.c` file, so everything it names is already in scope and there is nothing to guard against.
- FFI wrapper parameters are also prefixed, i.e. `ffi_rray_axis_names(r_obj* ffi_x, r_obj* ffi_axis, r_obj* ffi_frame)`. This frees up the unprefixed name for the converted C value.
- Headers only declare internal C functions, not FFI wrappers.
- `src/init.c` uses `extern` declarations for FFI functions — does not include feature headers.
- `#include "decl/{name}-decl.h"` always goes last, after every other include, separated from them by one blank line.
- Always prefer rlang's C library wrappers over raw R API (e.g., `r_globals.na_int` over `NA_INTEGER`, `r_length()` over `Rf_length()`).
- Prefer `r_ssize` over `int`, `r_length()` over `Rf_length()`, `r_attrib_get()` + `r_null` checks over `Rf_isArray()`.
- Any `r_obj*` returned by a C function that allocates must be protected with `KEEP()` / `FREE()` (rlang's wrappers around `PROTECT()` / `UNPROTECT()`) if used after any further allocation could occur.
- Before calling any C change done: for every new or touched `r_obj*` in the diff, name the function it is next passed to or read by, and check whether that function allocates before it protects or consumes the value. A value is not safe just because it looks used immediately — the callee's own allocations count. Do this as an explicit, separate pass over the diff, the same way `clang-format` is a separate mandatory step, not something folded into "look over the code once."
- Never use `gctorture()` or `gctorture2()` to look for protection problems. They are far too slow to be practical, even on a single call. The reading pass described above is the check.
- When looping over a vector, obtain a pointer to the underlying data first, e.g., `const int* v_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(dimensions)`, then index into that directly.
- Mark variables as `const` where possible.
- Never change code inside of `src/rlang/`, that is a vendored library.
- Always run `clang-format -i src/*.c src/*.h` after generating C code. Always run it over all files, not just changed files.

## R package development

### Key commands

```
# To run code
Rscript -e "devtools::load_all(); code"

# To run all tests
Rscript -e "devtools::test()"

# To run all tests for files starting with {name}
Rscript -e "devtools::test(filter = '^{name}')"

# To run all tests for R/{name}.R
Rscript -e "devtools::test_active_file('R/{name}.R')"

# To run a single test "blah" for R/{name}.R
Rscript -e "devtools::test_active_file('R/{name}.R', desc = 'blah')"

# To redocument the package
Rscript -e "devtools::document()"

# To check pkgdown documentation
Rscript -e "pkgdown::check_pkgdown()"

# To check the package with R CMD check
Rscript -e "devtools::check()"

# To format code
air format .
```

### Coding

* Always run `air format .` after generating code
* Use the base pipe operator (`|>`) not the magrittr pipe (`%>%`)
* Don't use `_$x` or `_$[["x"]]` since this package must work on R 4.1.
* Use `\() ...` for single-line anonymous functions. For all other cases, use `function() {...}`

### Testing

- Tests for `R/{name}.R` go in `tests/testthat/test-{name}.R`.
- Helpers for `tests/testthat/test-{name}.R` go in `tests/testthat/helper-{name}.R`.
- All new code should have an accompanying test.
- If there are existing tests, place new tests next to similar existing tests.
- Strive to keep your tests minimal with few comments.
- Never put code in a `test-{name}.R` file outside of a `test_that()` block.
- Avoid `expect_true()` and `expect_false()` in favour of a specific expectation which will give a better failure message. A few expectations in newer releases that you might not know about are `expect_all_true()`, `expect_all_equal()`, and `expect_r6_class()`.
- When testing errors and warnings, don't us `expect_error()` or `expect_warning()`. Instead, use `expect_snapshot(error = TRUE)` for errors and `expect_snapshot()` for warnings because these allow the user to review the full text of the output.
- Avoid the `.package` argument to `local_mocked_bindings()`; this modifies the namespace of another package which is not good practice. Instead create a mockable version of the function in the current package. See `?local_mocked_bindings` for more details.

### Documentation

- Every user-facing function should be exported and have roxygen2 documentation.
- Wrap roxygen comments at 80 characters.
- Internal functions should not have roxygen documentation.
- Whenever you add a new (non-internal) documentation topic, also add the topic to `_pkgdown.yml`.
- Always re-document the package after changing a roxygen2 comment.
- Use `pkgdown::check_pkgdown()` to check that all topics are included in the reference index.
- Always include a full blank line between `@param`s.
  expect_identical(as.vector(rray_add(Inf, NaN)), Inf + NaN)
})

test_that("can split along a single axis", {
  x <- array(1:24, c(4, 3, 2))

  out <- rray_split(x, 3)
  expect_length(out, 2)
  expect_equal(dim(out[[1]]), c(4L, 3L, 1L))
  expect_equal(dim(out[[2]]), c(4L, 3L, 1L))
  expect_equal(out[[1]], array(1:12, c(4, 3, 1)))
  expect_equal(out[[2]], array(13:24, c(4, 3, 1)))

  out <- rray_split(x, 1)
  expect_length(out, 4)
  expect_equal(dim(out[[1]]), c(1L, 3L, 2L))
  expect_equal(out[[1]], array(x[1, , ], c(1, 3, 2)))

  out <- rray_split(x, 2)
  expect_length(out, 3)
  expect_equal(dim(out[[1]]), c(4L, 1L, 2L))
  expect_equal(out[[1]], array(x[, 1, ], c(4, 1, 2)))
})

test_that("can split along multiple axes", {
  x <- array(1:24, c(4, 3, 2))

  out <- rray_split(x, c(1, 2))
  expect_length(out, 12)
  expect_equal(dim(out[[1]]), c(1L, 1L, 2L))

  out <- rray_split(x, c(1, 2, 3))
  expect_length(out, 24)
  expect_equal(dim(out[[1]]), c(1L, 1L, 1L))
})

test_that("splitting with integer(0) axes returns list(x)", {
  x <- array(1:6, c(2, 3))
  out <- rray_split(x, integer())
  expect_equal(out, list(x))
})

test_that("dimension names on non-split axes are preserved", {
  x <- array(1:6, c(2, 3), dimnames = list(c("a", "b"), c("x", "y", "z")))

  out <- rray_split(x, 1)
  expect_equal(dimnames(out[[1]]), list("a", c("x", "y", "z")))
  expect_equal(dimnames(out[[2]]), list("b", c("x", "y", "z")))

  out <- rray_split(x, 2)
  expect_equal(dimnames(out[[1]]), list(c("a", "b"), "x"))
  expect_equal(dimnames(out[[2]]), list(c("a", "b"), "y"))
  expect_equal(dimnames(out[[3]]), list(c("a", "b"), "z"))
})

test_that("dimension names on split axes are subset", {
  x <- array(1:6, c(2, 3), dimnames = list(c("a", "b"), c("x", "y", "z")))

+args <- commandArgs(TRUE)
+pkgload::load_all(args[1], quiet = TRUE)
+work <- "/private/tmp/rray-centralize-check.98vPHg"
+label <- args[2]
+round <- as.integer(args[3])
+set.seed(1041)
+if (round == 1L) {
+  results <- list()
+  for (i in seq_len(300)) {
+    ndim <- sample.int(5L, 1L)
+    dims <- sample(0:3, ndim, TRUE)
+    dx <- ifelse(sample(c(TRUE, FALSE), ndim, TRUE), dims, 1L)
+    dy <- ifelse(sample(c(TRUE, FALSE), ndim, TRUE), dims, 1L)
+    axes <- sort(sample.int(ndim, sample.int(ndim + 1L, 1L) - 1L))
+    x <- array(rep(c(1, NA, 2, 3), length.out = prod(dx)), dx)
+    y <- array(rep(c(1, 2, NA, 4), length.out = prod(dy)), dy)
+    z <- array(rep(1:2, length.out = prod(dims)), dims)
+    dimnames(z) <- lapply(dims, function(n) as.character(seq_len(n)))
+    results[[i]] <- list(
+      rray_add(x, y), rray_subtract(x, y), rray_multiply(x, y),
+      rray_divide(x, y), rray_exponentiate(x, y),
+      rray_broadcast(x, dims), rray_sum(z, axes), rray_prod(z, axes),
+      rray_split(z, axes), rray_split_names(z, axes)
+    )
+  }
+  if (label == "before") {
+    saveRDS(results, file.path(work, "reference.rds"))
+  } else {
+    stopifnot(identical(results, readRDS(file.path(work, "reference.rds")))))
+    cat("300 randomized cases across 10 operations match HEAD\n")
+  }
+}
+make_arr <- function(dims, type) {
+  v <- rep(c(1, 2, 3, 4), length.out = prod(dims))
+  v <- switch(type, dbl = v, int = as.integer(v), cpl = as.complex(v))
+  array(v, dims)
+}
+cases <- list(
+  vector = list(4000000L, 4000000L),
+  scalar = list(4000000L, 1L),
+  wide_axis0 = list(c(100L, 40000L), c(1L, 40000L)),
+  wide_axis1 = list(c(100L, 40000L), c(100L, 1L)),
+  tiny_axis0 = list(c(2L, 2000000L), c(1L, 2000000L))
+)
+out <- list()
+for (case in sample(names(cases))) {
+  types <- if (case %in% c("vector", "scalar")) list(c("dbl", "dbl")) else {
+    list(c("dbl", "dbl"), c("int", "int"), c("int", "dbl"), c("cpl", "cpl"))
+  }
+  for (type in types) {
+    x <- make_arr(cases[[case]][[1]], type[1])
+    y <- make_arr(cases[[case]][[2]], type[2])
+    gc()
+    timing <- bench::mark(rray_add(x, y), iterations = 25L, check = FALSE, memory = FALSE)
+    out[[length(out) + 1L]] <- data.frame(label, round, case, type = paste(type, collapse = "+"), ns = as.numeric(timing$median) * 1e9 / length(x))
+  }
+}
+x <- make_arr(c(100L, 40000L), "dbl")
+y <- make_arr(c(1L, 40000L), "dbl")
+gc()
+t <- bench::mark(
+  broadcast = rray_broadcast(y, dim(x)),
+  sum = rray_sum(x, 1L),
+  split = rray_split(x, 2L),
+  iterations = 20L, check = FALSE, memory = FALSE
+)
+for (i in seq_len(nrow(t))) {
+  out[[length(out) + 1L]] <- data.frame(label, round, case = c("broadcast", "sum", "split")[i], type = "dbl", ns = as.numeric(t$median[i]) * 1e9 / length(x))
+}
+write.csv(do.call(rbind, out), file.path(work, paste0(label, "-", round, ".csv")), row.names = FALSE)

void (SET_STRING_ELT)(SEXP x, R_xlen_t i, SEXP v) {
    if(TYPEOF(CHK(x)) != STRSXP)
	error("%s() can only be applied to a '%s', not a '%s'",
	      "SET_STRING_ELT", "character vector", R_typeToChar(x));
    if(TYPEOF(CHK(v)) != CHARSXP)
       error("Value of SET_STRING_ELT() must be a 'CHARSXP' not a '%s'",
	     R_typeToChar(v));
    if (i < 0 || i >= XLENGTH(x))
	error(_("attempt to set index %lld/%lld in SET_STRING_ELT"),
	      (long long)i, (long long)XLENGTH(x));
    CHECK_OLD_TO_NEW(x, v);
    if (ALTREP(x))
	ALTSTRING_SET_ELT(x, i, v);
    else {
	SEXP *ps = STDVEC_DATAPTR(x);
	FIX_REFCNT(x, ps[i], v);
	ps[i] = v;
    }
}

SEXP (SET_VECTOR_ELT)(SEXP x, R_xlen_t i, SEXP v) {
    /*  we need to allow vector-like types here */
    if(TYPEOF(x) != VECSXP &&
       TYPEOF(x) != EXPRSXP &&
       TYPEOF(x) != WEAKREFSXP) {
	error("%s() can only be applied to a '%s', not a '%s'",
	      "SET_VECTOR_ELT", "list", R_typeToChar(x));
    }
    if (i < 0 || i >= XLENGTH(x))
	error(_("attempt to set index %lld/%lld in SET_VECTOR_ELT"),
	      (long long)i, (long long)XLENGTH(x));
    if (ALTREP(x))
        ALTLIST_SET_ELT(x, i, v);
    else {
        FIX_REFCNT(x, VECTOR_ELT_0(x, i), v);
        CHECK_OLD_TO_NEW(x, v);
        SET_VECTOR_ELT_0(x, i, v);
    }
    return v;
}

r_obj* ffi_rray_split(r_obj* ffi_x, r_obj* ffi_axes, r_obj* ffi_frame) {
  struct r_lazy error_call = {.x = ffi_frame, .env = r_null};
  return rray_split(ffi_x, ffi_axes, rray_args.x, error_call);
}

r_obj* rray_split(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* axes,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  check_unclassed(x, arg, error_call);
  x = KEEP(arg_as_array(x, arg, error_call));

  r_obj* x_dimensions = KEEP(rray_dimensions(x, arg, error_call));
  const int* v_x_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(x_dimensions);

  const int dimensionality = rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(x_dimensions);

  axes = KEEP(arg_as_axes(axes, dimensionality, rray_args.axes, error_call));
  const int* v_axes = r_int_cbegin(axes);
  const r_ssize axes_size = r_length(axes);

  // Splitting 4x3x2 on axis 3 gives 4x3x1 out dimensions
  r_obj* out_elt_dimensions = KEEP(rray_set_axes_dimension(
    v_x_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_axes,
    axes_size,
    1
  ));
  const int* v_out_elt_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(out_elt_dimensions);

  r_obj* axes_complement =
    KEEP(rray_axes_complement(v_axes, axes_size, dimensionality));
  const int* v_axes_complement = r_int_cbegin(axes_complement);
  const r_ssize axes_complement_size = r_length(axes_complement);

  // Splitting 4x3x2 on axis 3 gives 1x1x2 split dimensions
  r_obj* out_dimensions = KEEP(rray_set_axes_dimension(
    v_x_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_axes_complement,
    axes_complement_size,
    1
  ));
  const int* v_out_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(out_dimensions);

  const r_ssize x_size =
    rray_size_from_dimensions(v_x_dimensions, dimensionality);
  const r_ssize out_elt_size =
    rray_size_from_dimensions(v_out_elt_dimensions, dimensionality);
  const r_ssize out_size =
    rray_size_from_dimensions(v_out_dimensions, dimensionality);

  const enum r_type type = r_typeof(x);

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_list(out_size));

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
    r_obj* out_elt = r_alloc_vector(type, out_elt_size);
    r_list_poke(out, i, out_elt);
    r_attrib_poke_dim(out_elt, out_elt_dimensions);
  }

  struct rray_iterator2 it;
  rray_iterator2_init(
    &it,
    x_size,
    v_x_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_elt_dimensions,
    dimensionality
  );

  switch (type) {
  case R_TYPE_logical:
    rray_split_lgl(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_integer:
    rray_split_int(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_double:
    rray_split_dbl(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_complex:
    rray_split_cpl(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_raw:
    rray_split_raw(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_character:
    rray_split_chr(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_list:
    rray_split_list(x, out, &it);
    break;
  default:
    r_stop_unreachable();
  }

  r_obj* names = KEEP(rray_split_names(x, axes));

  if (names != r_null) {
    r_obj* const* v_names = r_list_cbegin(names);
    r_obj* const* v_out = r_list_cbegin(out);

The regression tests pass, and 300 randomized cases across ten operations match the pre-change code exactly. Compiler diagnostics still show vectorized inner loops.

The first timing pass also puts the short-run integer case within about 1% of the original. I’m repeating the timings to check the remaining variation in mixed-type and complex arithmetic before committing.

r_obj* rray_reduce(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* axes,
  bool na_rm,
  rray_reduce_fn_switch fn_switch,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  check_unclassed(x, arg, error_call);
  x = KEEP(arg_as_array(x, arg, error_call));

  const r_ssize axes_size = r_length(axes);

  r_obj* out_dimensions = KEEP(rray_set_axes_dimension(
    v_x_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_axes,
    axes_size,
    1
  ));
  const int* v_out_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(out_dimensions);

  const r_ssize x_size =
    rray_size_from_dimensions(v_x_dimensions, dimensionality);
  const r_ssize out_size =
    rray_size_from_dimensions(v_out_dimensions, dimensionality);

  struct rray_iterator it;
  rray_iterator_init(
    &it,
    x_size,
    v_x_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_dimensions,
    dimensionality
  );

 b=statistics.median(v['before']); a=statistics.median(v['after']); print(k, f'{b:.3f} -> {a:.3f} ({100*(a/b-1):+.1f}%)')

{"comments":[{"id":"IC_kwDOUHAoJs8AAAABS4qt-w","author":{"login":"DavisVaughan"},"authorAssociation":"OWNER","body":"## Grid benchmark: does the runs optimization only help axis 1?\n\nShort answer: **no**. Swept `rray_add()` across vector size, element type, operation (runs vs. non-runs), and which axis is being broadcast. Every configuration benefits, and the win isn't concentrated on any one axis.\n\n### Part A — shape × broadcast axis (5M `dbl` + `dbl`)\n\n`tall` = large axis 0 / small axis 1 (long runs, few carries). `wide` = small axis 0 / large axis 1 (short runs, many carries) — the shape built specifically to stress the carry path.\n\n| shape | broadcast | ns/element |\n|---|---|---|\n| wide | axis0 | 0.788 |\n| vector | scalar | 0.750 |\n| tall | axis0 | 0.747 |\n| wide | none | 0.743 |\n| tall | none | 0.724 |\n| vector | identity (no broadcast) | 0.717 |\n| tall | axis1 | 0.706 |\n| wide | axis1 | 0.656 |\n\nWorst to best is only a ~1.2x spread. The shape purpose-built to maximize axis-1 carries (`wide`) isn't meaningfully worse than the shape that minimizes them (`tall`).\n\n### Part B — `add()` [runs] vs `subtract()` [no runs], identical shapes\n\n| shape | broadcast | add (ns/elt) | subtract (ns/elt) | speedup |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| tall | axis0 | 0.759 | 2.208 | 2.91x |\n| vector | identity | 0.738 | 2.210 | 3.00x |\n| tall | none | 0.731 | 2.283 | 3.12x |\n| vector | scalar | 0.710 | 2.237 | 3.15x |\n| wide | axis0 | 0.749 | 2.369 | 3.16x |\n| tall | axis1 | 0.689 | 2.275 | 3.30x |\n| wide | none | 0.718 | 2.395 | 3.34x |\n| wide | axis1 | 0.649 | 2.379 | 3.66x |\n\nEvery shape/broadcast combination gets a solid win (2.9x–3.7x). There's no configuration where the runs version regresses relative to the non-runs baseline.\n\n### Part C — type sweep, at the fastest and slowest shapes from Part A\n\n| shape | broadcast | type | ns/element |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| wide | axis0 | lgl+lgl | 1.108 |\n| wide | axis0 | int+int | 1.073 |\n| wide | axis0 | int+dbl | 0.890 |\n| wide | axis0 | dbl+dbl | 0.750 |\n| wide | axis0 | cpl+cpl | 1.347 |\n| wide | axis1 | lgl+lgl | 1.111 |\n| wide | axis1 | int+int | 1.098 |\n| wide | axis1 | int+dbl | 0.682 |\n| wide | axis1 | dbl+dbl | 0.645 |\n| wide | axis1 | cpl+cpl | 1.341 |\n\nType matters more than shape does. `dbl+dbl` is fastest, `cpl+cpl` slowest (16 bytes/element, as expected). Side finding, unrelated to the runs work: `int+int` is *slower* than `dbl+dbl` despite half the memory footprint — `rray_add_int_one()` runs an overflow check on every element, and `int+dbl` (which promotes to double and skips that branch) is measurably faster than pure `int+int`. Worth keeping in mind if `subtract`/`multiply`/`divide`/`exponentiate` get the runs treatment later.\n\n### Part D — size sweep at the worst shape from Part A (`wide`, axis0, `dbl`+`dbl`)\n\n| size | ns/element |\n|---|---|\n| 2e5 | 0.837 |\n| 1e6 | 0.787 |\n| 4e6 | 0.731 |\n| 2e7 | 1.256 |\n\nSmall sizes pay more fixed per-call overhead; very large sizes (2e7 elements = 160MB per array) hit real memory-bandwidth limits. Neither is specific to the runs design — any implementation hits this curve eventually.\n\n### Conclusion\n\nThe runs optimization isn't axis-1-specific. Absolute speed is driven far more by element type (double vs. int vs. complex) and total data volume (cache/bandwidth) than by which axis is broadcast or how many carries a shape forces. Non-runs `subtract()` is consistently ~3–4x slower than runs-based `add()` across the entire grid, with no weak points found.\n\n<details>\n<summary>Benchmark
